CONTENT WARNING
Within the presentation of Innsmouth’s locations, people, and history are various aspects that some Keepers and players may find
distressing, most notably incest, suicide, violence against children, abortion, animal cruelty, discrimination and racism regarding
some NPC attitudes, historical mention of the trade in forcibly enslaved people, and a lack of diversity within the town at large.
While such content is predominantly “off screen,” the Keeper should consider and discuss such matters with their group
before play begins, enabling the Keeper to adjust their presentation of Innsmouth to maintain everyone’s comfort levels,
including their own. Innsmouth is historically a predominantly white and conservative town—your version doesn’t have to
be, so change it to suit your vision.
Adjusting aspects problematic for your play group is easy to do, whether by toning such aspects down, removing any overt
depiction, or changing them entirely. Presentation is key, especially in a horror game. Understanding where to cut or to draw
a veil and fade to black is a useful skill for the Keeper to employ so that it doesn’t diminish anyone’s enjoyment of the game.

12

an overview of innsmouth
Captain Marsh Arrives
While Marsh found the local people unattractive, he was
greatly enamored by their unusual gold jewelry, which was
predominantly fashioned in the semblance of aquatic marine life.
Their chieftain, Walakea, indicated they were willing to trade such
bijouterie for the salt pork, silk fabrics, and cheap trinkets brought
by Marsh and his crew. Thus, Marsh acquired a sizable amount of
the distinctive gold jewelry for next to nothing. Marsh pressed
Walakea about the gold’s source and was told that Walakea’s
people were blessed by the “sea gods,” beings who could provide
more gold, if asked for it.
Marsh played to Walakea’s vanities in a bid to gain his trust
and favor. In return, Walakea took Marsh to a nearby volcanic
atoll to view yet more strange sculptures up close, explaining
that these were once parts of “the city of the sea gods beneath
the waves,” risen from the ocean floor in ancient times. Marsh
did not flinch when given a graphic demonstration of how
Walakea’s people offered up human sacrifices to these grotesque
carvings in return for good fishing, safe weather, and the ocean’s
golden treasures. If the sea gods were pleased, Walakea’s people
prospered. Marsh was keen to prosper too.
Upon departing Motuatua, Marsh promised to return,
bringing more of the Western goods the islanders sought. In
return, Walakea agreed his people would not trade with other
Westerners. Thus, the Innsmouth men set sail for home.
Over the next decade, Marsh continued the profitable South
Chief Walakea
Pacific trade, keeping his destination—Walakea’s island—a
closely guarded secret. He visited many other South Sea

Innsmouth lies on the coast of the Atlantic Ocean, straddling
[O] 1927–1928 (Winter): prompted by Olmstead’s report, the Manuxet River, within Essex County, Massachusetts. Nestled
the Treasury Department conducts a “strange and secret” within the Great Salt Marsh of eastern New England, the town

devastating raid on Innsmouth, freeing it from the control
narrow valley gorge, with tributaries running into the main
of the EOD and the deep ones, although the town is all
river, creating rapids and waterfalls at points along the river—
but destroyed in the process.
such waterfalls once powered the town’s mills in its heyday.
Hemmed in by the marshes, Innsmouth is quite isolated and
feels remote despite its relatively short distance to towns like
Arkham and Bolton.
Called the Great Innsmouth Salt Marsh by the locals, these
marshes and wetlands surround Innsmouth, a vast expanse of
reeds and sandbanks inhabited by terns and gulls.
Despite the scarcity of arable farmland, early colonizers
managed to find and clear some meager ground for planting. By
the 20th century, these early farms are nearly all abandoned, their
fields returned to sandy wastelands ravaged by wind and erosion,
the result of unchecked deforestation. Without the surety of
farming, the townsfolk had to look to the sea for their livelihoods.
Shipping, fishing, and mills served Innsmouth for some 200
years, although such trades have ebbed and flowed like the tide.
M
The town’s harbor, once the home port of globe-spanning
trade ships, is now clogged with eroded soil and sand washed
downstream by the Manuxet. Thus, the harbor can now only
be entered safely by the smallest of craft.
M
North of the harbor is a rocky spit of land, pierced by sea caves,
which extend out into the ocean.
21

chapter 1
M
Due east, more than a mile (1.6 km)
out to sea, lies the long dark line of
Devil Reef , a low rise of black stone
jutting irregularly above the waves. It
also is the site of numerous sea caves.
FINDING INNSMOUTH
Innsmouth can be reached via “improved
roads” (gravel tracks) off the state
highway, and by coastal track.
M
From the north: take Highway 1A
south from Newburyport to Ipswich ,
past Rowley, where an unsigned
improved road leads to Innsmouth. A
lesser known and rarely taken route
from Old Town follows a coastal
track, through the Great Innsmouth
Salt Marsh , to eventually arrive in
Innsmouth.
M
From the south: there are two roads
into Innsmouth. The main, most
direct route is from Highway 1A
through Ipswich , where an improved
road leads to Innsmouth. The second,

Prohibition
M
While Prohibition is in effect across the state, it has little
changed the consumption of alcohol in Innsmouth.
M
Establishments like the Garden Bar & Grill (601) openly serve
booze unimpeded by law enforcement. Human townspeople
generally tend to drink more alcohol than hybrids, although
this doesn’t mean that there aren’t hybrids who also seek solace
in inebriation.
M
No bootleggers operate stills within the town. Instead, its saloons
and homes are catered to through regular deliveries by those
bootleggers willing to come to Innsmouth. Recently, Danny
O’Bannion ’s Lucky Clover Cartage Co. (see Arkham, page
126) has begun making weekly deliveries. Thus, one could say,
Innsmouth is something of a hotbed of alcohol consumption.

SECRETUM MARIS: REVISED EDITION
Note: the original edition of this book, handwritten by Prosper Harmon, is in the possession of Eldridge Gilmer (The Clamshack,
910). All references to Harmon and his contributions in creating the EOD have been removed from the “official” version held at
the Esoteric Order of Dagon (212).
This leather-bound, handwritten book is actually a copy of
the original scripture, precepts, and gospel of the Esoteric
Order of Dagon. Said original was written entirely by
Prosper Harmon and its Latin title means “secrets of the
sea.” It incorporates the lore of the Deep Ones (page 38)
as taught to Obed Marsh by Walakea, and from Marsh’s
subsequent experiences with them in Innsmouth. Marsh’s
grasp of deep one theology was sketchy at best, and Harman
then reinterpreted it to create a palatable sense of religious
legitimacy for the newly founded EOD.
The book tells of Great Cthulhu imprisoned under the sea,
of the deep ones’ gods known as Father Dagon and Mother
Hydra (see Who is Dagon?, page 31), and the vast oceanic
cities of the deep ones. It has a vague parable of human
lands sinking into the sea, though few readers are likely to
realize this is a reference to Atlantis. Despite its many flaws
as a theological and historical work, it contains spells and
rituals enabling practical communication with the deep ones.
Secretum Maris (Revised)
Handwritten English, Obed Marsh, 1846

FINANCING
The primary source of the EOD’s income is the Marsh Refining
Company (507). In the early days, shipping and mill industries
boomed in Innsmouth, but as the years passed such businesses shrank,
and these industries either failed or diminished to almost nothing.
Fishing has always been strong, but while the town once
distributed catches across New England, this commerce has also
shriveled and now only a few fish-selling businesses within Essex
County come to collect the catches. Indeed, much of Innsmouth’s
catch now goes to feed the town and stave off starvation.
These days, the Marsh refinery operates at a fraction of
its former capacity. As a business, it ensures it possesses the
necessary trade licenses and pays the appropriate taxes to avoid
unwanted state and federal eyes from taking too close a look at
its operation. Profits from the refinery are poured into the EOD,
which enjoys certain historic tax breaks and exemptions.
Of course, all the gold the refinery claims to have purchased from
overseas mines derives from the deep ones (see A Golden Industry,
page 15). Should anyone look closely at the documentation held

the shadow over innsmouth
DEEP ONE HISTORY
Primordial History
Early in Earth’s history, approximately one
billion years ago, a relatively small number of
elder things arrived and colonized the planet,
where they evolved for flight, life on land,
and life underwater. The elder things found
the planet’s oceans were rich in organic
compounds, and as was their wont, they
tinkered with these raw ingredients of life
to create self-sustaining food sources. Such
biological manipulations led to the evolution
of all native terrestrial and aquatic species.
Through this process, the elder things
created, perhaps accidentally, the genetic
threads that would eventually evolve into
human beings and deep ones. As the two
genetic seeds were created from the same
materials, at the same time, they shared many
biological similarities. Thus began a process
of parallel evolution that lasted hundreds of
millions of years.
Later, Great Cthulhu came to Earth from
Xoth, accompanied by lesser beings, and
reared great cities of massive constructions
unintelligible to humans. Disputes over
territories, resources, and incompatible
ideologies led to conflicts between the elder
things and Cthulhu and its ilk. Wars were
waged on land, air, and sea. The elder things
deployed creatures called Shoggoths (page
206)—biologically engineered masses of
sentient protoplasm able to perform tasks as
well as fight on their behalf. The shoggoths
proved devastating to Cthulhu’s star-spawn.
Finally, Cthulhu was forced into a state of
suspended animation beneath the deep South
Pacific in the corpse city of R’lyeh; Cthulhu’s
star-spawn were similarly imprisoned for an
indefinite time.
The shoggoths, however, were not content
to remain their creators’ pliant servants. They
eventually revolted against the elder things,
and in a massive uprising, nearly destroyed
the elder things in their entirety. A small
surviving population of elder things fled to
their great city in the Antarctic, where, over
time, they, too, entered a state of hibernation.
Ancient History
The life created on Earth by the elder things
thrived, giving rise to a variety of plants and
animals through each phase of the planet’s
geologic and biologic history. About six
million years ago, deep ones evolved from their
marine ancestors, while early hominid human
ancestors appeared some five million years ago.
Cthulhu Falls to Earth
While human populations and civilizations
developed upon the land, deep one civilization
grew beneath the waves. In the Western
3399

chapter 2
hemisphere, the largest deep one
population center lay off the coast of
Africa, near what are now the Azores (see
The Atlantean Disaster, page 42). In the
Eastern hemisphere, the main center was
in the Indian Ocean, near what is now
Indonesia.
While imprisoned, Cthulhu was by no
means dead. Over the eons, it gathered
strength and reached out by psychic
means to both humans and deep ones,
the latter being far more susceptible to
the influence of Cthulhu’s dreams. Such
dream-influence shaped the deep ones’
ideology and beliefs.
DEEP ONE BIOLOGY
Deep ones evolved to have incredibly
slow metabolisms. Unless they suffer
sudden accidental/violent death, they are
practically immortal. Like some reptiles
and fish, they grow constantly, though
at a steadily decreasing rate, meaning
the oldest deep ones are also the largest.
To compensate for their longevity,
deep ones reproduce in small numbers,
giving birth to only a few young.
Following The Atlantean Disaster
(page 42), their birth rates fell below
self-sustaining levels. The introduction
of human genetic material produced
viable offspring, with deep one genes
ultimately expressing themselves after
individuals underwent a metamorphosis
in adulthood.
The Deep One Look
Deep ones’ appearance can vary greatly.
M
In general, deep ones are fish-like
humanoids, with most able to walk
on two legs—although some are
more comfortable scurrying about
on all fours.
M
While many appear grayish green in
color, some are more blue-white, while
others are paler.
M
Often coloration has evolved in
accordance with the deep ones ’ home
oceanic region.
M
Deep one scales tend to alter color in
different light conditions, and, like a
snake’s, seem shiny and slippery, but
are in fact dry to the touch.
M
Many deep ones have scaled ridges
running from the base of their heads
down their backs.
M
The majority have short, rudimentary
tails, although some specimens possess
Deep One, Variant
long and prehensile ones capable of
grasping objects.
4400
40

the shadow over innsmouth
A deep one’s physical shape is more or
less anthropoidal, although there can be
significant differences.
M
While four limbs are common, there are
those who boast multiple tentacular limbs.
M
Some appear to possess no bones, being
more like invertebrate jellyfish. Those
possessing such greatly divergent forms
usually never leave the sea.
M
Many, though, possess heads resembling
fish or frogs, most notably due to their
large, orb-like eyes.
M
The majority have gill slits on their
necks, while others have gills on their
torso, just above the lower abdomen.
M
All those who possess arms and legs
display webbed hands and toes, with
fingers ending in sharp claws.
In aquatic environments, deep ones
breathe by means of water passing
through their gill slits and over their gills,
where oxygen dissolved in the water is
exchanged for carbon dioxide in a manner
akin to that of fish. The downside to this
system is that they are susceptible to
hypoxic water (lacking oxygen) or water
that has been contaminated by natural or
human-caused toxins.
While their ancestors could only
survive on land by breathing through
their skin and the lining of their mouths
and throats, modern deep ones have an
advantage. Because they metamorphosed
from human beings, they still have vestigial
lungs, allowing them to breathe more easily
when out of the water. Adult deep ones can
live on land, but prefer to live underwater.
“Hybrids”
Couplings between deep ones and humans
require two consenting participants for
fertilization to complete. Like some
fish and frogs, deep ones can be serially
hermaphroditic, meaning an individual can
change from male to female and back again
if circumstances warrant. Deep ones are,
therefore, capable of breeding with human
beings of either biological sex.
As in humans, embryos develop inside
the female deep one’s body, nourished—to
some extent, at least—by their mother’s
blood. The exact mechanisms are unknown
due to a lack of scientific study. Both
species give birth to live young.
Occasionally, though, for reasons
unknown, a deep one or human parent
births an egg containing the hybrid baby,
Deep One, Variant
in a manner similar to a monotreme (e.g.,
a platypus or echidna). This egg typically
hatches within 24 hours of being laid. The

chapter 2
Communication
M
Over the course of their long,
evolutionary process, deep ones have
developed a form of communication
like telepathy .
M
When on land, deep ones revert to
using their vestigial vocal apparatus,
using croaking-sounding speech, as
well as physical gestures.
M
In addition, deep ones communicate
through “R’lyeh Glyphs,” a system
of writing able to convey meaning
and information to humans and deep
ones alike.
The storage of information is
important to deep ones, and, over the
millennia, they have developed an ability
to breed corals whose form, color, and
shape become a repository of knowledge.
Thus, such “gardens” of phosphorescent
and strange leprous corals are akin to
human libraries and jealously guarded.
R’lyeh Glyphs
Also known as R’lyehian, R’lyeh glyphs
are a hieroglyphical text consisting of
“word” bars—usually horizontal, but
sometimes vertical—from which single
letters hang down to form words and
phrases—the letters are best characterized
as being swirling or spiraling designs,
seemingly more akin to artists patterns
than recognizable letters. Deep one-made
stone tablets, jewelry, and other artifacts
sometimes include lines of such glyphs.
Pronunciation, by those able to
decipher the language, seems to consist
of glottal stops where pressure is placed
on the glottis, closing, and pivoting the
vocal folds, producing what sounds like a
stream of guttural consonants. The effect
is quite bizarre and unnerving to hear,
with language experts unable to identify
or trace its phonological heritage.
Artisans
A tradition of soft metalworking results
in intricately carved white- and yellow-
gold jewelry, with such items often worn
by religious leaders or else used to entice
humans into trade agreements. Made of
naturally occurring alloys found in great
quantities beneath the sea, the metal is
Deep One, Variant
hand formed and hammered, carved, and
chased, without the aid of heat or flame.
4444

the shadow over innsmouth
Principal Deep One Societies
Deep ones live in disparate regions
and, consequently, have developed into
diverse societies, with differences in
physical attributes and behaviors—the
Keeper may use these differences to
confound their players’ expectations
as they see fit. The following locations
reflect the largest concentrations of
deep ones on Earth, but there are
undoubtedly some not too far away from
most maritime human communities.
North Atlantic
M
These deep ones are descended from
those who were involved in the
destruction of Atlantis .
M
Their own city destroyed, they crossed
to the western side of the North
Atlantic and established Y’ha-nthlei
in the deep waters off Devil Reef ,
near what would one day become
Innsmouth, Massachusetts.
South Pacific
M
Epipelagic deep ones, this colony
arose principally from survivors of
The Atlantean Disaster (page 42)
who migrated to distant waters near
what is now the island nation of the
New Hebrides (Vanuatu), west of
Fiji. Here, the migrant population
joined with deep ones long-
established in the South Pacific.
M
As this group live nearer to the
surface, due to the comparatively
shallow waters of the archipelago,
they were the first to interact and mate
with humans following the Atlantean
debacle. The process proved successful
and has been sustained for centuries.
Abyssal (Deep) Ones
M
Also located in the South Pacific, this
colony dwells in the deep waters of
the Mariana Trench—a 1,580 mile
(2,543 km) long trench in the Western
Pacific.
M
They have adapted to the immense
pressures, stygian darkness, and frigid
temperatures in the ocean’s extreme
depths. They have the largest eyes of all
deep ones and have evolved a form of
echolocation to assist with hunting prey.
M Deep One, Variant
Culturally, they are the furthest
removed from other deep ones , only
interacting with other populations on
rare occasions.
4455

chapter 2
M
They are not known to have any regular
interactions with human beings, and their
numbers are dwindling.
Erythraean Deep Ones
M
The largest colony of deep ones in the
Indian Ocean, located in a deep-water
trench east of the Chagos Archipelago
which is, in turn, due south of the
Maldive Islands.
M
Like their Pacific and Atlantic brethren,
they have resorted to mating with humans
to achieve sustainable birth rates.
M
While they maintain some unique
cultural practices, they have been known
to dispatch emissaries to both the Pacific
and Atlantic deep one colonies.
Hyperborean Deep Ones
M
This community lives in the northern
regions of the Barents Sea, beneath the
polar ice cap.
M
Like abyssal ones, they have adapted to exist
in very cold waters. They rarely emerge above
the ice, except during some ritualized hunts.
M
Professor William Channing Webb
reported encountering a group of
“esquimaux” on an 1860 expedition to
the northwestern coast of Greenland, and
these Indigenous people claimed to have
“husbands and wives who lived joyously
as fish in the sea below the ice.”
M
It’s believed this polar community of
deep ones is substantially smaller than
those in other parts of the world.
Of Humanity
Deep ones interact with humans to obtain
the genetic material they need to ensure
the survival of their species. Whether they
desire or need human sacrifices is open
to interpretation, but they are willing to
accept such grisly tributes from their human
associates. Most deep ones find humans
pitiful and slightly revolting.
Between the 1860s and 1900, Innsmouth’s
deep ones largely accomplished their key
goal to ensure the survival of Y’ha-nthlei
though the birthing of Hybrids (page 41).
After Captain Obed Marsh died in 1878,
their generosity in ensuring abundant
fishing and plentiful gold waned. As a result,
Innsmouth’s financial fortunes have ebbed.
Many fisherfolk get by, but no longer prosper.
The Marshes are the richest family in
town, but the Marsh Refining Company
is not a thriving concern. By the 1920s,
Innsmouth is an incredibly bleak shell of
what it once was. It has all the grand old
Deep One, Variant mansions from the mid-19th century, but very
little new significant investment in the town.

USING INNSMOUTH IN GAMES
Innsmouth as a setting works equally well for one-shot and campaign games.
M With one-shots, the full terrors of the town might be brought to the fore in a “one night of horror” type game. This is fine,
given the reason that the players only get to experience Innsmouth once or, at least, there will be a large interval before
the players, possibly with different investigators, return to its shadow-haunted streets.
M For campaign-style games, where the investigators are encountering Innsmouth and its inhabitants in an on-going basis, we
recommend that you grow the horror slowly; resist throwing everything the town can offer in the early stages of such games.
The trick with presenting Innsmouth is to avoid it becoming a “one-trick pony” and instead slowly peel layers away from the
town, revealing its secrets little by little, building the horror and revelation to a suitable climax. In a campaign, especially one
where the investigators are based out of Arkham, Innsmouth should be a question mark in the minds of the players.
They may come to know that the place is shunned but not necessarily know why. Initial encounters with Innsmouth might focus
on information to be gained from the town, information that can serve to help with cases in Arkham and its surrounds, such as
finding a diary, artifact, or book that contains some useful clue, but one that does not necessarily add to the lore of Innsmouth.
Such initial forays might see the investigators dealing with Innsmouth folk, who could be humans or Hybrids (page 41).
Remember, many hybrids look perfectly normal or just a little “odd.”
Hybrids who are undergoing the change into becoming a Deep One (page 38) tend to remain out of sight, so encountering
these can wait till later in the campaign, once the Keeper decides to start peeling away the town’s layers and reveal its secrets.
In so doing, the Keeper may confound player expectations—some players may have read Lovecraft’s The Shadow Over Innsmouth
or seen a movie concerning the town and so expect to see deep ones and horrors literally wandering the streets at midday.
By focusing on things other than the deep ones and the Esoteric Order of Dagon (EOD) (page 30), the Keeper changes things
up, and the players might be lulled into thinking Innsmouth (in the game) is different to what they expected. The investigators
might find allies among the hybrids; after all, as long as their activities don’t trouble the EOD or delve too far into the town’s
history and secrets, they won’t necessarily run afoul of the locals. They may get the sense, or be told, that they aren’t wanted
in town, but this should simply hint of things to come later in the campaign.
Space out visits to Innsmouth and build on the investigators’ knowledge bit by bit. In time, find ways to pull them back, using
relationships made with Innsmouth folk or otherwise. Eventually the web of Innsmouth drags the investigators back and draws
them into its full horror.
Alternatively, check out the various options presented in The Deep Ones’ Agendas (page 47); these options provide quite
different motivations and will, naturally, confound those players who believe they know everything there is to know about Innsmouth!

ALTERED SKILLS
This section describes new and revised skills related to
Innsmouth.
Navigate (Innsmouth) (% varies)
This revised skill is pertinent only for campaigns set in
Innsmouth, Arkham, and the wider Miskatonic Valley. This
option allows investigators to have the skill specialization of
Navigate (Innsmouth) with a starting base value determined by
their association with the town:
M
Born and raised in Innsmouth: starting base skill 40%.
M
Born in Innsmouth, but has lived elsewhere for most of their
life: starting base skill 20%.
M
Born elsewhere, but has lived in Innsmouth for up to one
year: starting base skill 1D10+10%.
M
Born elsewhere and has only just moved to Innsmouth:
starting base skill 1D6+1%.
This skill embodies a character’s knowledge of Innsmouth
in terms of finding their way around the streets and knowing
where important buildings are located. In all respects, it acts like
the standard Navigate skill, just focused down to Innsmouth’s
geography. While Innsmouth is much smaller than Arkham, it
once boasted a population of over 2,000 and, although there are
now many abandoned or run-down buildings, a person can still
get lost, especially at night.
M
Success: a character gets to where they want to go without
delay, with higher levels of success possibly meaning they find
a shortcut or make the journey faster than expected.
M
Failure: may mean getting delayed for a time or even
sidetracked.
M
Fumble or Pushed Failure: could mean getting lost and
stumbling into a “bad” situation, such as running into a dispute
of some kind, seeing “something” they are meant to see, or
simply arriving at the wrong address.
Of course, a Keeper should only call for a Navigate roll when
it’s dramatic—when time is of the essence or when locating
the correct address is of paramount importance (e.g., breaking
into the wrong house could result in a nasty surprise!).
Additionally, this skill can act as a shorthand for an
NPC’s general knowledge of Innsmouth—where’s where
and who’s who. Although one should generally use the
Lore (Innsmouth) specialization (nearby) when dealing
with knowledge of the town’s past.
Additionally, certain NPCs detailed in Chapter 4: Guide to
Innsmouth (page 77) have the skill Navigate (Salt Marshes),
which refers specifically to the land surrounding Innsmouth—
this is intended to clearly demonstrate certain NPC’s knowledge
of the land around the town. For investigators, just use the
Navigate (Innsmouth) skill.
Note: this skill may be too much fuss for some, who may prefer
Two Investigators
6600

to simply use the standard Navigate skill. If this is the case,
ignore this specialization for investigators.
NEW SKILLS
Lore (Innsmouth) (% varies)
Innsmouth lore is a measure of how much a person knows about
the town’s history, secrets, and way of life. This specialization
is pertinent only to residents of Innsmouth, Arkham, and the
Miskatonic Valley.
This skill embodies a character’s knowledge of Innsmouth’s
history, its people, customs, secrets, the ability to recall certain
significant names, and so on. Commonly known information (see
The Public History of Innsmouth, page 12) may not require a
roll or, if necessary, a Regular roll at most, while more esoteric
or secret information would require a Hard or Extreme success,
although some secrets will be impossible to know until discovered
“in game” (see What is Known?, nearby).
M
Success: can usually recall one piece of useful information about
Innsmouth, such as a name, a place, or piece of history.
M
Failure: indicates either a lack of relevant knowledge or the
inability to recall the information.
M
Fumble or Pushed Failure: may mean the information recalled
is incorrect (e.g., a lie told to the investigator). This sets the
investigator down a wrong path or has them make a wrong
assumption that could prove troublesome or dangerous later
(e.g., tipping off an enemy).
The starting base value of the skill for investigators is
dependent on their association with the town:
M
Born in or has lived in Innsmouth for at least 10 years:
starting base skill 20+1D10%.
M
Born elsewhere: starting base skill 01%.
M
Born elsewhere but lived in Innsmouth for up to one year:
1D10+10%.
What Is Known?
Broadly speaking, the Keeper should use the following
banding as a guide when relating information from the
use of this skill—look at a NPC’s Lore (Innsmouth) skill
to determine the level (band) of knowledge they possess;
thus, an NPC with Lore (Innsmouth) 30% might know
some of the names of Innsmouth’s elite, but does not
know enough to talk in detail about these families nor
any secrets concerning the Esoteric Order of Dagon
(page 30).
M
01–10%: common knowledge of Innsmouth, elements of
its public* history.
M
11–30%: the full public* history of Innsmouth, general
knowledge of who’s important.
M

Copies of the Innsmouth Courier
Several decaying and fragile issues dated 1840–1846. These
contain articles and editorials by the editor, John Lawrence,
which describe the mysterious disappearances taking place in
Innsmouth, as well as alleging that Captain Obed Marsh and
members of his Esoteric Order of Dagon are the perpetrators—
see Innsmouth Courier, page 67.

His diary explicitly describes Captain Obed Marsh and his
followers’ roles in several midnight journeys to Devil Reef and
of alleged human sacrifices made there.

Danvers State Hospital
Danvers, Massachusetts
Opened in 1878, the hospital (aka Danvers State Insane Asylum)
provides residential treatment and care for 500+ patients with
mental health issues, as well as nurse training programs and a
pathology laboratory.

TABLE 2: GENERAL GOSSIP
Gossip
1 They say in the early 1800s that old Captain Marsh discovered a pirate hoard out on Devil Reef.
I hear there’s a gang of moonshiners working out of the ruined houses north of the river. Best to stay clear of ‘em as
2
they’re a ruthless and cutthroat mob.
3 Those in Innsmouth covet golden jewelry and keep it hidden away.
4 Old Obed Marsh’s second wife wasn't from around here. Several of Marsh’s sailors also had foreign wives.
5 Those old sailors brought back the plague from their travels. Swept right through Innsmouth in the 1840s and many died.
I heard that, a few years back, a factory inspector got a terrible scare in Innsmouth. Apparently, he’s now a permanent
6
resident in Danvers’ sanitarium.
My dog growls whenever we pass the Innsmouth bus stop. She don’t like them who wait there. I heard all animals shun
7
Innsmouth.
There’s plenty of abandoned houses in Innsmouth. Some say they aren’t really abandoned and are used as hideouts by
8
criminals lying low or else by hoboes and squatters.
Don’t go poking your nose in Innsmouth’s cellars! There’s more than just saltwater and damp down there! And nothing
9
good Christian folk should concern themselves with.
My father told me the Innsmouth lot ran their priests and churches out of town. That they’d forsaken God and turned to
10
paganism.
11 Old Marsh built a new church in Innsmouth and preached weird mumbo-jumbo alongside his sermons.
The Innsmouth fisher people know their waters well, but that coast’s dangerous and likely to rip your nets and batter
12
your boat ‘less you know where to avoid the black rocks under the water.
About the time Obed Marsh started his new religion, something like the Order of Dragon or Dagon, the land around
13
Innsmouth became infertile. Maybe it was God punishing Innsmouth’s people for their blasphemies.
The fishing off Innsmouth is supposed to be very good, but those there guard the rocks. There’s plenty of tales of
14
outsiders being threatened unless they move on.
15 Nowadays, they say there’s only fish packing keeping Innsmouth going. Oh, and that refinery too.
16 There’s weird things lurking in the salt marshes north of Innsmouth.
17 The refinery takes in ore and refines gold, shipping out across the world. I’ll bet there’s some rich folk in Innsmouth.
18 Folks in these parts won’t marry into Innsmouth. They say the plague still lingers in their blood.
There’s only one hotel apparently. The Gilman Hotel or as like. Although it’s said to be haunted, with strange noises
19
keeping folks awake at night.
Since the Civil War, the government have sent people to Innsmouth. But ain’t anything ever come of it. My grandfather
20
said the government people left in a hurry and never went back.
72

investigating innsmouth
Gossip
21 There can’t be more than a dozen businesses still operating in Innsmouth these days. The place is dying.
22 That Marsh family has run Innsmouth since back in the 1830s. Captain Marsh’s children run things now.
I hear that some of the old Innsmouth warehouses have long-forgotten cargo stored in them. Some might have been
23
stored and left alone for over a hundred years. Who knows why or what they’re keeping.
24 Don’t you be going to Innsmouth! People go there and don’t come back.
25 There can’t be more than 300-400 people left in Innsmouth. Over half the town is abandoned and falling to ruin.
Legend says someone cursed the Gilmans. Apparently, in the early 1800s, the Gilman family’s shipping trade made
26
them rich until they ran into a series of disasters. Ships were lost to storms and misfortune.
There’s plenty of folks who’ve disappeared near Innsmouth. Could be bootleggers, robbers, or worse. Who knows what
27
goes on in the salt marshes around that cursed place.
28 The town’s cursed by old man Marsh’s sin. They say that Marsh sold his soul to the Devil for gold.
TABLE 2: GENERAL GOSSIP
29 Out beyond Innsmouth is Devil Reef. Full of twisted rocks and murderous waves.
30 It wasn’t all Captain Marsh’s idea. Cooked it up with mate, Prosper Harmon, and then had him killed.

A handful of residents are likely to be seen on the street
during daylight hours while in this neighborhood; most either
work at the Dockery Brothers Distillery (104) or frequent
the Innsmouth Sailors’ Society (105) or The Mermaid
(106). Outsider-inspired disturbances draw more folk,
most exhibiting some variation and degree of the
Innsmouth Look (page 42). Visitors would do well
not to tarry long in the area and are advised to
ignore any untoward sounds they might hear
coming from abandoned and seemingly
empty houses.

chapter 4
SCRIMSHAW
Powers
Also known as scrollwork, scrimshaw are artifacts created
by sailors—primarily whalers—from the bones and teeth of Exactly what Bradley’s Bone does is left to the Keeper. Some
whales and other marine mammals like walruses. Such bones possibilities include:
are handcrafted, engraved with often elaborate and artistic
designs, usually of a maritime nature (e.g., ships, portraits, M Protection or warding, granting safety from deep ones,
naval scenes). The artwork is enhanced using pigment to who cannot approach within 10 feet (3 m).
ink and color the designs. This can serve as a form of artistic M Sea breath, granting the ability to breathe underwater for
pursuit, a way to spend time on long voyages, and a means to a time (suggest 1D4 hours).
create gifts or saleable items. M Luck boon, conferring one bonus die to Luck rolls.
While the vast majority of scrimshaw is purely decorative,
there could well be a few, rare examples of such teeth or bones
While some form of benefit appears to affect its owner,
holding some form of magic; they may be infused by the creator
Bradley’s Bone is also cursed:
of the piece or else due to the fact the bone does not come from
a whale, but rather some Mythos creature. Such pieces may M Deep ones can sense its presence within 100 yards/meters.
protect from or repel certain Mythos entities or perhaps allow M Its power(s) do not affect hybrids (i.e., they are not repelled
spell-casters to store and use magic points to better power
by it).
their magical workings.
Certainly, such pieces are exceedingly rare and sought after M All those within 6 feet (2 m) of the owner/holder suffer one
by wizards and cultists alike. penalty die to all Luck rolls. This is continuous until the Bone
is cast away or given to another.
BRADLEY’S BONE
Bradley’s Bone is one example of a Mythos-infused piece of
scrimshaw. In this case, it is the tooth of a rather large deep one.
No one knows who Bradley was, and most assume him
to have been a whaler in the 17th century due to the legend
that has arisen regarding the item. While many have heard
the tale, most don’t hold it to be true, and no one has actually
seen the item, although some old sailors claim to have seen
and held it. Such folk are usually half-cut with rum, so their
stories are unreliable at best. Thus, where Bradley’s Bone is
now remains a mystery.
The tale of the bone posits that Bradley was a whaler out
of Nantucket (sometimes New Bedford) who caught a “whale
that was not a whale.” The creature was said to be of great size
and thought at first to be a sperm whale, yet, on inspection, it
was like no whale the sailors had ever seen. With huge arms
and legs, and massive head, it seemed more akin to a merman
of tremendous proportions.
The ship’s crew thought the creature was an ill omen and
wanted to cast it off and let the deep take it, but before this
could be accomplished, Bradley jumped on the carcass and
returned with one of its great teeth. Despite the mutterings
of the crew, Bradley kept the tooth and spent his time working
it, fashioning a design that was said to be a likeness of the
creature where he got it. The tale goes that the ship struck
something on its voyage home and all but one hand was lost.
The survivor being Bradley.
Whether by luck or charm, Bradley’s Bone saved him from
a watery death.
Danger

Jerome Bicknell
104 DOCKERY BROTHERS DISTILLERY age 34, distillery operator
29 Main Street
STR 70 APP 55
Jerome Bicknell and his wife Edna Bicknell (nee Dockery) CON 70 POW 60
operate this distillery. Running an illicit business that’s
SIZ 60 EDU 70
sandwiched between the EOD and Danny O’Bannion’s gang
(Arkham: Lucky Clover Cartage Co., 412) has hardened the DEX 55 SAN 48
Bicknells and inured them to violence. INT 85
While Jerome runs the operation of the business, his wife Edna
is the cook. The distillery’s principal output is their Marsh Gin,
also known as “Deck Wash” (“decks awash” was a genuine slang HP 13

is occasional fraternization with the hybrid workers. Generally,

maintains a healthy business, fundamentally delivering the same
service as B-W-M Fish-Packing Co. (109). With around 40
workers, the business does not boast a cannery; instead, it focuses
on fresh seafood delivery to Arkham, Newburyport, and Dracut.
Hybrid workers are in the majority, with only a handful of
humans employed. While technically in competition, the two
firms co-exist peacefully and co-operate to ensure both operate
successfully, which means agreeing on fixing prices, sharing
resources when required, and so on.

1–19 Fish Street
The buildings here were once a bustling hive of activity, but now
lie dormant and abandoned. Looking like decrepit versions of
those farther along Fish Street, these sheds and factory buildings
serviced the fishing trade in years past, but are casualties of
Innsmouth’s economic downturn.
Still reeking of fish—the smell carries over from the still
operating fish-packing plants—the buildings are dark, damp,

Ralsa attended Harvard Law School but, despite his father’s
influence, was expelled during his second year. The younger Marsh’s
poor attendance and even poorer grades, and his interest in less
scholarly subjects—notably his instructors’ daughters—were his
eventual undoing at the school. Thus, Ralsa returned to Innsmouth
to squander his time while waiting for his father’s inevitable death,
at which time he stands to inherit the family estate.
Ralsa is a notorious ladies’ man, responsible for fathering
several illegitimate children in Innsmouth and the wider
Massachusetts area. Thus, Ralsa’s hybrid progeny could potentially
be encountered in Arkham, Dunwich, Cambridge, or even
Kingsport. Some of his children appear human, others more
like Innsmouth’s hybrids, while others may be something else
entirely. In consequence, Ralsa is hated by many in town, but
being one of the Marshes, few dare to raise a word against him.
He recently overstepped his bounds when he tried to woo Patricia
Averill, a human woman of a proud local family. Miss Averill and
her family were not pleased by Ralsa’s unending attention, which
was more akin to stalking than romancing. Mr. Quentin Averill
(Averill Residence, 404) has sworn harm upon Ralsa should be
ever go near his daughter again. The Innsmouth Marshes have now
warned Ralsa to mend his ways, lest he be cast out of the family.
Look to the Future
Ralsa is incapable of changing for the better. He continues to
pursue women with the aim of finding a wife. If he accomplishes
this, he continues his philandering ways, much to the agony of his
wife and wider family. At some point, his wife, a former partner,
or someone like Quentin Averill will decide enough is enough
and “deal” with him, permanently. Thus, either Ralsa might seek

Closed since 1846, when rioters destroyed most of the paper’s
History
archives. Captain Obed Marsh and his followers had long been
the target of the paper’s editor, John Lawrence, whose editorials  Built in 1814, it was abandoned during the early 1840s when
condemned Marsh’s worship of false idols and accused him and  Captain Obed Marsh and his followers ran the priest, Thomas
his followers of kidnapping, and worse.  McCarthy, out of town.
Such editorials played a role in fueling local attitudes to
Inside
Captain Marsh, who was subsequently arrested and jailed—an
event that instigated a riot, with the deep ones and Marsh’s  A musty smell greets those venturing inside. The rows of carved
followers destroying large parts of the town and killing many  wooden pews remain mostly intact, although some nearer the front
people in the process. John Lawrence disappeared that night,  have been overturned or otherwise smashed, littering the floor with
along with most of the newspaper’s journalists, press operators,  debris. An ornate wooden crucifix that once hung behind the altar
and other employees. All never to be seen again.  now lies on the floor, covered in dust, while the stone pulpit has
been smashed to little more than a pile of rocks. Above, bats nest
in the ceiling; if disturbed, the bats come to life, flapping around
the interior like a mini-whirlwind. A wrecked door leads down to a
small crypt that housed the remains of four former pastors or elders.
A search of the building finds its records have been destroyed
or else removed, leaving little but rat and bat droppings.

This massive stone building was built in the 1830s. Although
a sign still identifies this as the Congregational Church, it is
STR 50* APP 0
a wing of the EOD, having been taken over when the parson
CON 0 POW 60 fled the town in the mid-1800s. Like other local churches,
SIZ 60 EDU 45 the sermons now reek of blasphemous notions about bodily
— immortality through the worship of Father Dagon. The churches
DEX 30* SAN
in neighboring towns have disavowed the place, with accusations
INT 70*
of heathenistic and pagan leanings.
The church is of Gothic Revival design, with a basement and
two-story tower with broken clocks. The many basement windows
HP 13 are shuttered, yet the cracked bell is rung every hour between
DB 0 sunrise and sunset. This ugly, crumbling edifice is referred to
Build 0 by some human residents as “St. Toad’s,” but only in whispers.

chapter 4
(1) Entranceway
Inside, it is a dingy place; skylights in the roof of the upper floor
have not been cleaned for years, so the light emanating from
these is dismal and green-tinged due to lichens. The entranceway
contains four imposing marble pillars reaching up to the ceiling
of the upper floor. Hanging between the pillars are time-worn
and ragged Masonic pennants.
Beyond the pillars, to the rear are two flights of steps leading
up to the upper floor, while behind and beneath these are a
second pair of steps down to the basement floor. Above, a second-
floor balcony overlooks the scene.
At the far rear are three doors: the one on the left leads to a
toilet (3), while the middle door (4) opens into a short corridor
to an external rear door:

A successful Cthulhu Mythos roll identifies the subject of the
sculpture as an idol of Great Cthulhu. If touched, it emits a
sudden freezing sensation, which fades slowly over the course
of several minutes, but provokes a Sanity roll (0/1D2 loss).
M
In addition, if they fail a POW roll, the person in contact with
the idol has a momentary vision of a murky, undersea city of
great basalt columns and strange-angled structures, all covered
in seaweed. At the Keeper’s discretion, the person revives from
the vision with a mouth full of seawater.
(11) Robing Chamber
Stairs from the Meeting Hall (9) lead into a room upon whose
walls are a series of brass hooks, on which hang a plethora of
resplendent blue-green hooded robes—each is adorned with a
single icon, woven in silver-colored thread. Below the hanging
robes are a series of wooden footlockers, with most of these
locked by a padlock.
The arched ceiling is hand-painted, depicting a view of an
underwater city showing a community of coral and rock formed
into homes and buildings, graced by waving fans of corals, sea
vegetation, and other marine life. In the background a hulking,
dark shape of disturbing proportions can be seen.
M
A successful Cthulhu Mythos roll suggests the mural on the
ceiling shows a deep one city, perhaps Y’ha-nthlei, and the
hulking shadow upon it as that of Father Dagon (see Who
is Dagon?, page 31).

wood and debris make these places dangerous to explore.
In the better parts, architectural styles range from collapsing
Northern Residential 400–499
17th-century houses to decaying gambrel-roofed Georgian homes.
The outer blocks tend to have small and overgrown yards, with
the houses built close together. The streets are generally unpaved,
The Northern Residential neighborhood lies on the northern and with no streetlights or sidewalks. The nicer blocks, west of
western sides of Innsmouth, bordered by Main Street and Rowley Lafayette Street, display typical middle-class hip-roofed Federal
Road to the east and west, and Boundary and Dock Streets to properties, many dating to the late 18th and early 19th centuries,
the north and south. In contrast to the “modern” streets of the built by Innsmouth’s early industrialists. Many of these homes
Central Residential (page 125) district, the properties here tend have shuttered attic windows, permanently sealed against the
to be older and, consequently, more rundown and dilapidated, outside world.
although nearer to Dock Street in the south, the homes appear There are fewer uninhabited homes in this area and sidewalks

401 POORHOUSE & ORPHANAGE

human inhabitants, who eke out a meager living by doing
76 Federal Street
whatever jobs they can get. A few hybrids live in the slum area

402) rule the roost here, with the other human residents keeping out by the Newburyport Road. Once a farm, it was converted
a wide berth to avoid feeling the Gorton’s wrath. to a poorhouse and orphanage in 1842. The property sits amidst
On the eastern end of the neighborhood are numerous abandoned sizable gardens, which once would have been a delight to behold,

The Summoning Spell
In 1848, Gardner Averill attempted to summon and bind a
demon. Earlier that year, Obed Marsh’s followers had murdered For his part, Quentin will not share his grandfather’s ritual
Gardner’s son, George, but despite being held for the crime, spell—or grandfather’s ritual flute—with anyone outside the
they were released without charge. Enraged, Gardner and his family, viewing it as a powerful heirloom that he cannot pass
remaining son, Arthur, father of Quentin Averill, worked together into the “wrong hands.”
to prepare and enact the summoning ritual in the hope that they
could command a demon to kill those who had slain George.
On Midsummer’s Eve, the pair successfully summoned and
bound something. Not a demon from the “other side,” but rather a
servitor of the Outer Gods! Despite their nausea and terror, they
mustered enough whit to order the creature to seek out and kill
George’s murderers—a task it completed with apparent relish.
The incident caused the Marsh family to quickly develop a
healthy respect for the Averills, extending this to their minions to
ensure the family was left alone. Indeed, subsequently, the EOD’s
members have avoided antagonizing the Averill household. Thus,
something of a truce exists—as long as the Averills do not bother
the EOD, the EOD will not bother them. That is, until Ralsa
Marsh came along…
Recent History
Ralsa Marsh (Dr. Rowley Marsh & Sons General Practitioner
& Legal Services, 203), a despicable womanizer, recently took
an interest in Quentin’s daughter, Patricia, attempting to woo her
into marriage despite Patricia’s disinterest. Ralsa became more

405 Gilman Residence
3 Backs Street
A small, two-story, somewhat shabby house on the western
edge of town. Cynthia Gilman (née Jenckes) lives here with
her second husband, George Gilman, along with five children.
Notable Folk
M
Cynthia Gilman, 42, human, weary and unhappy homemaker.
M
George Gilman , 36, hybrid, churlish husband (Gilman House
Hotel, 702).
M
Five children: Owen, 15; Sookie, 13; Marybelle, 10; Gladys,
8; and Roger, 6; all nasty and cruel.
History
Cynthia’s first husband, Clive (human), died a few years
after they had been married in a “workplace accident”—
apparently some heavy crates fell on him while working at
Billingham-Waite-Martin Fish-Packing Co. (109). Not
long after Clive had been buried, George Gilman turned up
and wooed Cynthia with promises of affection and financial
stability. George was the eldest son of Francis Murray Gilman,
owner of the Gilman House Hotel (702) and so came from a
prominent Innsmouth family.

chapter 4
While Barnabas Marsh (Marsh Mansion, 306) owns the
company, his son, Sebastian Marsh (S. Marsh Residence, 302),
manages it, who in turn leaves the day-to-day operations to his
son, Jacob Marsh (J. Marsh Residence, 307). Barnabas never
visits the refinery or its offices, and Sebastian does so only rarely.
Jacob can either be found here or at the office on Waite Street
(Marsh Refining Co. Office, 710).
When Jacob isn’t around, the workers turn away curious
outsiders, refusing to speak about the business. Important or
official-looking visitors are referred to the company office;
this includes state factory inspectors, who must be cleared by
management before they are allowed inside.
At night, an armed watchman, Robert Ballant, patrols the
factory. He shoots to kill, phoning for help if necessary.
Ground Floor
Shipping
This area holds a few large crates—containing old machine parts,
and, depending on the time, one to three stacks of gold ingots or
none at all. Two loading doors connect to a dock, where vehicles
can pull up to load and unload. A large freight elevator connects
to Sub-Levels 1 and 2, while two metal staircases—one by the
elevator, another on the opposite side to the dock—lead down
to Sub-Levels 1 and 2. At the southern side of the area is a
breakroom and some toilets.
Receiving
Contains the cyanide vat and its related equipment, plus the
conveyor belt leading to the building holding the grinder. None
of these mechanisms appear to have been used in years. The
cyanide vat holds about 48 inches (122 cm) of weak, pungent
solution, over which a reflective film has settled.

chapter 4
601 THE GARDEN BAR & GRILL
119 Washington Street
A sign above the door of this seedy looking place reads “The
Garden,” and includes a faded picture of a plate of meat and
potatoes next to a foam-topped mug of ale. The establishment
is open 10:00 am (or so) to 12:00 am, seven days a week. Once a
middle-of-the-road eatery, it is now nothing more than a saloon,
owned and operated by a rough-looking, German immigrant
named Victor Obrecht.
The place is dimly lit and smells of fish, stale beer, and smoke.
Although currently illegal due to Prohibition, the place operates
openly, unbothered by the local authorities, some of whom
number among the establishment’s best customers.
M
Shots of whiskey and mugs of warm beer cost 50 cents apiece.
The clientele includes hybrids and humans, although these
different groups tend to avoid one another and come in at
different times; humans tend to come here in the midmorning
to lunch, while hybrids can usually be found here in the late
afternoon to evening. The hybrid community are Obrecht’s best
customers, spending more than his human patrons.
Notable Folk
M
Victor Obrecht , 45, human, cigar-chomping owner.
M
The six employees of the Marsh Refining Company (507)
can sometimes be found in here: Robert Waite, Jackson
Pratt, Arnold Gates, Amos Sumner, Emanuel Peach, and
Tobia Cutler.
Obrecht’s Wisdom
Obrecht is a sullen man not normally prone to talking about
Innsmouth. He doesn’t like strangers coming in and asking
questions, especially questions about his background and history.
If offered something appealing, such as a bribe, he may be willing
to talk, as long as he can’t be overheard. Obrecht is a gambling
addict and finds it hard to resist a wager; thus, another option is
to figure this out and present him with a gambling opportunity.
When speaking, he whispers conspiratorially in a thick
German accent, perhaps offering the following wisdom:
M
The locals are very insular and don’t like strangers—he advises
investigators to avoid poking their noses into the town’s
affairs, as people go missing.
M
He came to town some five years ago from Germany and
bought the Garden for a bargain price from its former owner,
a man named Jock Casey, a former sailor turned diner owner.
M
Casey claimed he needed to sell quickly as he needed the
money for his sick mother who lived in California. He left
town immediately once he had Obrecht’s money and has not
been heard from since.
M
“Innsmouthers” suffer from a strange disease local to the area,
causing many to die young in their 20s and 30s.
M
Talk of “fish-devils” is “nonsense;” he’s never seen one!
M
If pressed on his background, Obrecht refuses to say any more
and turfs the investigators out; a successful Psychology roll
reveals he is afraid, not of the hybrids, but of revealing too
much about himself—see Obrecht’s Secret, nearby.

guide to innsmouth
A Conversation?
Whether in the aftermath of a fight or if
calmer heads prevail, Garrison may explain
what is happening. He lives here with his
wife, Dora, along with their three children:
Billy, Della, and Nora. The children are
kept locked in the cellar and in the attic
at night for their safety.
If pressed, he expounds, saying that the
children are kept this way to prevent them
being “infected by the Innsmouth disease.
You’ll have seen it, most folks around here
have it.”
The children are well-fed, bathed, and
cared for, but they aren’t let outside.
M
A successful Psychology roll detects
that Garrison—and Dora, if about—is
suffering from a deep-seated paranoia,
fueled by ignorance of the truth about
the Innsmouth Look.
M
Falling on hard times, Garrison took
up the offer of work at the Cabot farm
and moved his family to Innsmouth
just a year ago. After settling in, they
began to note the sometimes-unusual
appearance of their neighbors and
people about town. Somehow, Garrison
learned about the “plague” that affected
Innsmouth in the past and believes most
of the townsfolk are afflicted. Hence, he
and Dora give everyone a wide berth, and
keep their children inside.
M
Garrison is trying to save enough money
from the farmwork to move his family
out of Innsmouth.
If the investigators spend time with
the Garrisons, they could convince the
couple that their children are safe from
any imagined infection. Of course, less-
patient investigators may choose to remove
the children by force, or alert local or state
authorities about the situation. The local
police either ignore the matter entirely or,
at the Keeper’s discretion, hand the kids
over to the Poorhouse & Orphanage
(401)—perhaps realizing their mistake, the
investigators may be guilted into helping
the Garrisons rescue their children.
If the investigators contact state
authorities, they may investigate, although
it takes several months for them to act.

chapter 4
The pair are good friends of Daniel and
Sandra Mowry (Mowry Residence,
606), with Stephen and Daniel knowing
one another through their jobs,
while Beverly and Sandra usually
call upon each other to chat and
catch up on the town’s gossip.
Like the Mowrys, the Deans are
without children and have taken
the First and Second Oath.
The couple are quite active
supporters of the EOD, their
forebears being fishing families
who benefitted from Obed Marsh’s
new religion and subsequent
oceanic bounty. They attend services at
the Faith Baptist Church (213)—Stephen
has asked Reverend Ezra Hartley if he could
be put forward for the Third Oath (see The
Three Oaths of Dagon, page 36), but has
yet to hear if he’ll be accepted.
Should Daniel and Sandra
Mowry have any trouble with
out-of-town investigators, they
are likely to call on the help of
the Deans, who perhaps assist
to trap, hold, or escort wayward
investigators into the arms of the
EOD. In the meantime, the Deans
are capable observers, happy to spy
on the comings and goings of folks
in their neighborhood and secretly
report their findings to the EOD.

608 OBED MARSH MEMORIAL
Intersection of South, Marsh, & Johns Streets
A bronze statue of a 19th-century sea captain, some 10 feet (3
m) tall, including its raised plinth, dominates this triangular
patch of green situated in the middle of the intersection.
Surrounded by an iron-railed fence, the statue’s plaque notes:
Captain Obed Marsh
1804–1876
Erected in 1885 by the People of Innsmouth in
Recognition of the Marsh Family’s Generosity
The statue, railings, and the green are in surprisingly good
repair and are obviously regularly tended, unlike most things
in Innsmouth.
M
Standing close to the statue, one may notice that Obed ’s
nose is much shinier than the rest of the piece, suggesting
that it has been rubbed many times.

704 WAITE’S VARIETY STORE
6 Elliot Street
A dilapidated building with a dingy storefront, “Waite’s Variety
Store” is painted in faded lettering on the dirty and streaked glass
of the store-front window. The store offers all manner of items,
from stationery supplies to tools, curios to fireworks, and much
more. All packed into a small maze of crowded and narrow aisles.
For those in the know, bootleg liquor is sold from the back
room—the booze is purchased from Victor Obrecht (The
Garden Bar & Grill, 601). Open 9:00 am to 5:00 pm Monday
to Saturday.
The Proprietor
The store is owned and operated by Thomas Waite, a human,
who is married to a deep one wife and with whom he has a
hybrid daughter. The years have not been kind to Waite, and he
looks much older than he really is. Under most circumstances,
he doesn’t talk much with strangers as he fears the wrath of the
EOD. Thus, he barely acknowledges customers and is often found
staring at a small, tarnished locket containing an old picture of a
188

guide to innsmouth
M
pretty, young woman—his long-lost love, Clara Adams. Darold Eliot, 41, hybrid, cook, and bother of
Thomas (pictured right) was the younger brother Zelzora and Morgan Elliot, who prefers not to
of Ephraim Waite (E. Waite Residence, 304), who speak to the patrons.
M
passed away last year. He is aware of the rumors Brittany Eliot , 20, hybrid, daughter of Darold,
concerning his late brother, that of him being a and waitress, somewhat clumsy and forgetful.
wizard, but dismisses such talk as offensive. In Key skills: Charm 60%, Drop Your Order in
truth, Thomas was afraid of his elder sibling Your Lap 70%, Forget One Thing You Ordered
and avoided him as he was a domineering bully. 80%.

Thomas Waite (pictured right), 44, human, fearful
proprietor.
Rumors
For some reason, no one seems to know where the rumor
originated, some people whisper that Thomas Waite has a treasure
he keeps in a safe in the back of this store. Those spreading the
rumor suspect the treasure is old pirate gold, but it is something
much more valuable.
Inside the Safe: The Book of Dagon
Hidden in the back of the store is a small, sturdy, and heavy safe.
Inside are various business papers, the store’s weekly takings,
and a locked drawer.
If opened, the drawer contains a stack of yellowing,
handwritten papers, wrapped in a cloth—a translation of the
ancient stone tablets secretly kept in the basement of the
Esoteric Order of Dagon (212). Translated by Obed Marsh
from the R’lyeh Glyphs (page 44) on the tablets he brought
back from Motuatua (see The Secret History of Innsmouth,
page 12), the translation is titled the Book of Dagon.
Using knowledge learned from the Book of Dagon, Thomas
Waite has carved an Elder Sign on the inside of the safe’s door,
protecting it from deep ones and hybrids alike. Despite this
precaution, the Elders (page 33) of the EOD are aware that the
“book” is in Waite’s possession, and they wish to own it.
While previous attempts to find and steal the book have failed,

190

guide to innsmouth
Billingham-Waite-Martin Fish-Packing Co. (109) and
Eliot’s Fish-Packing House (110)—the businesses alternate
delivery days—as well as occasional fresh catches brought in
by independent fishing captains. A door beside the counter is
marked “O. Waite.”
Operating out of the back office, Orman Waite works to sell
Innsmouth’s seafood across the state. He takes a commission from
the fish-packing houses in town to promote and sell their catches,
as well as those from a handful of independent fishing ships that
still eke out an existence. Waite, a proud and shrewd operator, is
frequently out of the office, visiting existing customers or trying
to find new ones. Occasionally, his out-of-town customers may
come here to meet him.
Waite leaves the seafood counter to his two employees, Mary
Hale and Fannie Pierce, who open and lock up, serve customers,
and do whatever else is needed. When Waite is about, he does
at least help when a delivery arrives.

“outnumbering” attack after the first.
Grab (mnvr): a successful grab indicates one tenacle-finger
has wrapped around the target’s leg, arm, or body, with
each additional successful maneuver adding to the number
of constricting tentacles. A single tentacle has 2 hit points
(increase to 3 for lager specimens), and can be severed if
damaged, although the held person suffers one penalty die to
their attacks; if three or more tentacles hold the target, two
penalty dice are applied. A held person may attempt a STR or
DEX roll opposed by the demonic fungus’ STR to break free.
Crush: once held, the target is crushed, causing 1D2 damage
per turn per tentacle. Optionally, at the Keeper’s discretion,

chapter 4
SHOGGOTH LORE
Shoggoths were created by the elder things a billion
years ago. Monstrously large and strong, shoggoths are
infinitely adaptable, able to sprout limbs, organs, eyes,
or orifices at will.
Genetically engineered as servants, shoggoths built
the elder things’ cities and fought their wars against the
star-spawn of Cthulhu and the dreadful flying polyps.
Following these wars, the shoggoths developed greater
intelligence and, consequently, became resentful of their
servitude. Eventually, they turned on their erstwhile
overlords, virtually wiping the elder things from the face
of the planet.
Without the elder things to destroy, the shoggoths
became increasingly lethargic and passive, essentially
“hibernating” for unimaginably long periods of time. In
this torpid state, they are vulnerable to magical suggestion.
Over time, certain deep one colonies discovered
hibernating shoggoths and devised means to control
them. While such control is effective, it is not without
significant risk, as shoggoths are liable to rapidly turn
on their “controllers” if given half a chance. Thus, the
deep ones rarely use shoggoths to advance their plans.
Such monsters are kept back and only used as a last
resort. In effect, shoggoths are somewhat like nuclear
warheads—very dangerous weapons that must be treated
with respect and fear.
While humanity may occasionally cause the deep ones
to be tempted to deploy shoggoths against the upper
world, their use has remained restrained. The deep ones
are fully aware of the lessons concerning the fate of the
elder things. But, someday, the stars will be right, and
then, what devastation will the shoggoths wreak?
setting out at sea in their small dinghies, which are kept by their Option: Geologic Shoggoth

drifted through various jobs, from fish packing to crabbing, but

209

chapter 4
Eldridge Gilmer’s Secret
Eldridge believes Prosper Harmon (see The Secret History of
Innsmouth, page 12) was a true prophet of Father Dagon, and
so Harmon is venerated among the Gilmer clan. Eldridge is
one of the few EOD members who has not attained the rank of
Deacon, a fact that continues to bother him.
Eldridge secretly possesses the original manuscript of Secretum
Maris—see Secretum Maris: Unexpurgated Manuscript, nearby.
This priceless book is kept wrapped in cloth within a footlocker
hidden away in Eldridge and Eileen’s shack home.
The Gilmer’s most-prized possession, this book contains the
true history of the EOD and details Prosper Harmon’s role in
developing Innsmouth’s new religion. Eldridge’s grandfather, Silas,
saved the text from destruction at the hands of Obed Marsh, who
wanted to purge Harmon from history while shaping the EOD
to his own will (see Secretum Maris: Revised Edition, page 34),
resulting in an altered version of the book (the “revised edition”) .
Having studied the original text, Eldridge understands the
changes made by Obed Marsh and how the modern EOD
has drifted from its fundamental precepts. All the Gilmer
family realize that revealing this book’s existence could be very
dangerous.
Notable is Eldridge’s opinion that trafficking with outsiders
has been one of the many ways that the EOD has drifted from
its original purpose. As a result, he’s decidedly unwelcoming to
any non-Innsmouth people who come to his shack for clams—or
any other purpose.
Eileen Gilmer’s Zeal
Eileen truly believes humans are an inferior species, with the
deep ones being the highest life form imaginable. She has fully
embraced the Third Oath (see The Three Oaths of Dagon,
page 36).
M
Eileen has two sons with Eldridge: Obed and Prosper.
M
Eileen also has a deep one husband, “Boxer ,” with whom she
has had three children; of these, two were born in a condition
known as Blessed of Cthulhu (page 205), being so aquatic in
nature they needed to go to sea immediately.
M
Her third child with Boxer, Sarah-May, has, despite her young
age, rapidly developed the Innsmouth Look (page 42), suggesting
that she’ll go to sea at a younger age than most. Eileen is very
proud that Sarah-May is likely to go to the sea soon.
Like Eldridge, Eileen’s feelings of guilt have compelled her belief
that she has lived an “impure” and “land-leaning” life, and such
thoughts and actions have prevented her from being able to go to sea.
Eldridge & Eileen’s Children
Obed is the eldest and a prodigious clam-digging talent, due
to his slightly oversized left hand. By nature, he is furtive and
sneaky, likely to observe visitors to the clamshack before they see
him. Obed embraces his father’s hardline fundamentalist EOD
beliefs with enthusiasm.
Prosper is the younger and more extroverted of the Gilmer boys.
He aspires to rise through the ranks of the EOD and someday
return it to its former glories—his beliefs informed by his parent’s
stories of the old days and how Prosper Harmon’s vision has been
distorted by those who now run the Order. Thus, he feels the Order
The Old Lighthouse
has become too attached to politics, money, and worldly goods
and its connection with the sea, the deep ones, and even to Father
Dagon and Mother Hydra is weakening each year.
221100

guide to innsmouth
SECRETUM MARIS:
UNEXPURGATED MANUSCRIPT
This is the original Secretum Maris, as written by Prosper
Harmon around 1841–1843; it consists of handwritten,
loose pages tied together with a leather strap, wrapped
in cloth, and stored in an unremarkable and beaten-up
wooden box.
This version of the text contains none of Obed Marsh’s
alterations as found in the later revised edition. Instead, it
provides comprehensive guidance on using the practices
of Walakea on Motuatua in developing and maintaining
beneficial relations with the deep ones, as well as
knowledge of the deep ones, their beliefs concerning
Great Cthulhu, Father Dagon, and Mother Hydra (see
Who is Dagon?, page 31).
Throughout the text are religious teachings and
practices as designed by Harmon and Marsh—their
original ideas and concepts for the religion that would
be embodied in the EOD. Additionally, Harmon, as the
writer, often posits himself as central to the EOD’s mission
while also citing his own “immense powers,” while little
is written regarding Obed Marsh.
Secretum Maris (Unexpurgated)
Handwritten manuscript in English, by Prosper Harmon,
1841–1843
M
Sanity Loss: 2D8
M
Cthulhu Mythos: +4/+10
M
Mythos Rating: 42
M
Study: 22 weeks
M
Suggested Spells: Attract Fish,* Breath of the Deep,
Contact Deity/Cthulhu, Contact Deity/Father Dagon
or Mother Hydra†, Contact Deep One, Quicken the
Voice of the Deep,* Summon/Bind Deep One; others
at the Keeper’s discretion.
*See Grand Grimoire of Cthulhu Mythos Magic for spell
descriptions.
†See Appendix B: Marine Magic & Artifacts, page 252.
While Prosper Gilmer will protect the EOD’s secrets, he is
willing, perhaps even eager, to engage with visitors to Innsmouth
to discover what has brought them to town. In one sense, he
doesn’t think the EOD should be a limited religion and, rather,
its message could go wider; after all, if the EOD recruited more
people, it would become more powerful. Whether such youthful
notions come to cause issues for Prosper, the Gilmers, or the
EOD in general, only time will tell.
Eileen & Boxer’s Child
The Gilmers keep Sarah-May concealed inside the family shack
as her deep one-like appearance is so pronounced. Unlike most
hybrids, she began to change very early in life and has now
metamorphosed to a point where she is perhaps months or even
days from taking to the sea forever. Encouraged by Eldridge &
Eileen, Sarah-May is proud of being a “Chosen One,” ready

chapter 4
The Outskirts
221144

guide to innsmouth
At Night
Environs:
After dark, a green, barely discernible mist rolls over the cemetery
grounds while the night air is filled with the croaking of frogs.
The Outskirts OS100–OS199
The eerie mist, combined with moonlight and the overgrown
vegetation and angled grave markers, can conjure weird-
looking shadow-shapes, making the place seem haunted to the
THE SALT MARSHES
uninitiated. Sometimes, during fall, corpse lights—also known
The salt marshes around Innsmouth are a part of the larger as will-o’-the-wisps—may be spotted emerging from the graves.
Great Marsh of eastern New England. This land is a tidal marsh,
Notable Folk
regularly flooded by Atlantic waters, encompassing sand- and
M
mudflats, sandy dunes, rivers, islets, and islands. Salt-tolerant Otis Fuller , 47, human, alcoholic gravedigger (Fuller Residence,

OS112 TOWN DUMP
This stinking town dump is reached by way of a bumpy, rutted
road off the main Newburyport Road. Sunk into an area of
dunes, marshes, and streamlets, this contains Innsmouth’s raw
garbage. Scavenged by crows and gulls by day, at night the place
crawls with raccoons, possums, and rats.
222

guide to innsmouth
No one tends the site anymore. The last caretaker, a human The Smugglers' Tunnels - Keeper's Version
named Robin Buckshorn, died in 1907. Since then, the dump
has been left to fester.
OS113 FARTHING FARMHOUSE
Henry Farthing, a somewhat paranoid farmer, lives alone in
this old farmhouse, north of the Rowley Road. He just about
sustains himself on the vegetables and livestock he raises here.
Note: Henry Farthing appears in the scenarios “The Crawford
Inheritance” and “Old Acquaintance” in the forthcoming The
Curse of Innsmouth scenario collection.
OS114 SMUGGLERS’ TUNNELS
Along the sandy and rocky shores of the inlets northeast of
town are several sea-caves. This section only covers those caves
in the southernmost inlet, nearest the town, and northwest of
Kelly’s Cove.
The sea-caves were discovered by early Innsmouth sailors and
colonizers, who widened and extended them so that they reached
all the way underneath the northern portion of town. Ideal for
smuggling cargo into town, sections of the tunnel system lead
directly to the cellars of certain houses where wooden docks or
landings were constructed and remain still.

OS115 BOYNTON BEACH

226

guide to innsmouth
Boynton Beach is approximately 1 mile (1.6
km) long and is bordered by 50 feet (15 m)
high stone cliffs. Near the base of the cliffs
and nestled among sand dunes set back from
the tideline are bunch of shacks built from
driftwood and stone. Clearly, these shacks
have been here some time, and it’s evident
that there are people living here.
An old wooden quay runs along a section of
the beach, usually a handful of fishing boats
are moored here.
Notable Folk
M
Steve Welch, 43, grizzled fisherman and
leader.
Beach Community
Just 26 people, mostly human fisherfolk and
their families, make this place their home—
something of an offshoot of the nearby
Bass Ridge, although over time, this beach
community has become isolated and separate
to the people living in Bass Ridge.
These hardworking and simple people stay
away from Innsmouth and, when at sea, give a
wide berth to Devil Reef (OS118). They are
quite poor, but prefer to sell their catches to
the packing houses in Gloucester rather than
take them to the nearer and more convenient
ones in Innsmouth.
They instinctively mistrust outsiders and
prefer to scare or warn off strangers. Those
visitors able to make a good impression—
and make it known they are not from
Innsmouth—may be allowed a brief stay on
the beach. Should they be asked, everyone
earnestly warns outsiders to stay away from
Innsmouth, citing a highly contagious disease
that causes “malformations of the body.” It is
unlikely they will trust any outsider enough
to say more on the subject.
The unofficial leader of the community is
Steve Welch, a grizzled 43-year-old fisherman.
Like everyone else here, Welch warns strangers
away from Innsmouth and refuses to provide
aid, fearful that associating with strangers
could bring the displeasure of EOD (212)
down upon the people of the beach.
OS116 FISH-HEAD ROCK
Southeast of town on the Coastal Pathway.
A rocky crag extending into the sea, just
beyond the northern end of Boynton Beach
(OS115), resembling the face of a gigantic
fish or frog. Seemingly crudely carved into
the rock, it possesses a narrow head, wide
mouth, protruding eyes, and gill slits. While
the profile is tantalizing, the sea provides the
best way of seeing Fish-Head Rock.

wants a dead specimen to study, and if he can learn anything that for the investigators.
might be helpful, pass on this information to the investigators.
Possibilities
Perhaps, rather than a full corpse, Bloom asks for specific body
M
part (e.g., eye, head, claw, scale). The investigators could be in dire peril if they enact Bloom’s
plan—either immediately or later, when the EOD comes
Backstory
looking for them.
M
Doctor Bloom says he knows how to summon a fish man and If a deep one is captured or killed, how do the investigators get
where to do it: Fish-Head Rock (OS116) near Boynton Beach it to Doctor Bloom without arousing suspicion in Innsmouth?
